Generic 'test the cutoff' prompts produce one-sided tests that don't distinguish strict-greater from greater-or-equal.** — A test that evicts at 31 days proves the eviction works but doesn't pin whether 30 days exactly evicts or survives. The contract is in the comparison operator, and only paired tests pin it. Future refactors flipping `>` to `>=` should break a named test, not slip through. _(e.g. Step 7's `test_clear_older_than_evicts_at_exact_cutoff` (30d + 1s evicts) paired with `test_clear_older_than_keeps_run_at_boundary` (30d − 1s survives) pins `age > cutoff_seconds` to one second on each side.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via detail:run-log-structured-events#7</sub>
- **Path-traversal / security test coverage must distinguish argv-shape attacks (strings passed via CLI) from disk-shape attacks (symlinks, hardlinks, mount points already on disk). A `@pytest.mark.parametrize` over argv strings cannot reach the disk-shape cases; those need their own test functions with `tmp_path` setup.** — A guard that uses `Path.resolve()` is defending against two threat models at once (string-form traversal AND symlink-following). Tests that only sweep argv strings leave half the contract unpinned. Reviewers six months later assume parametrized coverage is total when it isn't. _(e.g. Step 6 parametrized `['../etc/passwd', 'demo/../escape', …]` over argv; step 7 added `test_show_refuses_symlinked_slug_dir` and `test_clear_slug_refuses_symlinked_slug_dir` which seed `<runs_root>/evil` as a symlink to outside the root.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via detail:run-log-structured-events#7</sub>
- **When a regression-only step's tests overlap with sibling-step tests, the detail's 'Why' section must name the overlap explicitly and defend it as intentional double-pinning (e.g. 'step N pins X in isolation, step M pins X under composition Y'). Silent duplication will be flagged as redundant in review and may get deleted, eroding the regression contract.** — Regression tests by definition guard against future refactors, which means they often re-test something a unit test already covers — but from a different angle (integration vs primitive, composed vs isolated). The defense is part of the test's documentation. Without it, the next reviewer can't tell whether the duplication is sloppy or load-bearing. _(e.g. Step 7's schema-mismatch test overlaps step 1's `test_read_in_flight_returns_none_on_schema_version_mismatch`; the detail's 'Why' explains step 1 pins the primitive in isolation while step 7 pins that `append_event` and `read_in_flight` stay decoupled under a schema-99 snapshot.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via detail:run-log-structured-events#7</sub>

- **For docs-only steps that thread a feature across multiple coordinated entry-point docs (README + RUNBOOK + SKILL, or equivalent), the detail's acceptance criteria should pin cross-references with literal `grep -F` checks (e.g. `README.md#section-anchor` appears in the other two files) so the trio can't silently drift.** — Three-file docs steps fail not by missing content but by one file forgetting to link the other two. Test runs can't catch this; only grep can. Without pinned cross-refs in the criteria, reviewers eyeball it and miss broken anchors. _(e.g. Step 8's criteria required `grep -F "README.md#run-logs"` and `grep -F "RUNBOOK.md#workflow-investigating-a-slow-run"` to hit in the sibling files — this catches drift that 'looks fine on skim' would miss.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via detail:run-log-structured-events#8</sub>
- **When a docs step exposes symbols/events/CLI subcommands frozen by prior implementation steps, the detail's acceptance criteria should require enumerative coverage ("mentions all N event kinds" / "all four subcommands appear in the Quick Reference") rather than narrative checks, so drift between code and docs is detectable by `grep -c`.** — Implementation steps freeze a closed set of symbols (event names, subcommand verbs, error codes). Docs steps that miss one create a silent contract gap — the user reads the README, never sees `iteration_end`, and assumes it's not emitted. Enumerative criteria turn this into a one-line grep check. _(e.g. Step 8 required the new README section to mention all 12 event kinds (`run_start`, `phase_start`, …, `run_end`); a simple `grep -cE '(run_start|phase_start|…)' README.md` confirms coverage.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via detail:run-log-structured-events#8</sub>
- **Docs-only steps with `complexity: low` are correctly rated as low *engineering* effort but often require high-precision placement (insert between `file.md:347` and `file.md:349`, between named H2s). The create proposal should — when files exist already — surface the closest two anchor symbols (previous H2 + next H2) as a hint, not just the filename.** — Detail spent most of its read budget locating exact insertion points between existing H2s. The placement decision is the load-bearing one (the section docks to an existing narrative arc); the prose is mechanical. Surfacing anchor symbols at create-time would shift that work left. _(e.g. Step 8's README insertion lives 'between `## Resuming after a failure` (ends 347) and `## Modes` (starts 349)' — the anchor pair is what reviewers need to verify placement, more than the filename.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via detail:run-log-structured-events#8</sub>

- **When a detail body mentions a test file (e.g. `tests/test_X.py`), that path must appear in the step's `files:` array alongside the source files it covers — not just in prose.** — Test-file drift is the single most common files-coherence finding: the design narrative names tests, but the machine-readable manifest only lists production code. The `files:` array is the source of truth for downstream tools (locator, review heuristics, ownership checks); leaving tests out makes the step look smaller than it is and breaks the coherence check. _(e.g. This review surfaced 20+ files-coherence findings, almost all of the shape "detail cites tests/test_*.py not in step.files" across steps 1–7. The fix is uniform: append the test path(s) to step.files when create or detail mentions them.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:run-log-structured-events</sub>
- **When the plan's subject is the tool/codebase doing the review (a self-referential plan), heuristic "already done" matches are mostly false positives — token overlap with existing helpers is structural, not evidence of completion.** — Plans that extend a tool inherit its naming conventions (`cmd_*`, `Run`, `claude_invoke`, `StderrReporter`), so almost every proposed new symbol grep-hits an existing one. Treating those INFO findings as real status signals leads reviewers to skip steps that are genuinely unimplemented. _(e.g. Five INFO findings on this review (steps 2, 3, 6, 8) flagged symbols like `cmd_create`, `cmd_checkpoint`, `StderrReporter`, and even the token `Run` in RUNBOOK.md — none of which prove the step is done; they just prove the plan touches the same module as existing code.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:run-log-structured-events</sub>
- **If every top-level body section (`## Overview`, `## Problem statement`, `## Architecture`, `## Risks`, `## Testing`, `## Acceptance criteria`, `## Deferred`) is still a `_(TODO)_` placeholder, review should short-circuit with a single "draft body first" notice rather than emitting one body-completeness finding per section.** — A plan with no body cannot be meaningfully reviewed for scope, risks, or acceptance criteria — yet today the tool emits one MEDIUM finding per placeholder section (8 here), which dilutes the higher-value findings (files coherence, step heuristics). One consolidated signal is more actionable than eight near-duplicates. _(e.g. This review produced 8 MEDIUM body-completeness findings, all of the form "`## X` body is a `_(TODO)_` placeholder". The reviewer's takeaway is the same regardless of count: the plan body was never drafted.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:run-log-structured-events</sub>

- **When a detail body names a concrete file path (especially under `tests/`), that path must also appear in the step's `files:` array — if create didn't propose it, detail must add it back rather than leave it dangling in prose.** — Files-coherence drift was the dominant failure here: every implementing step (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8) had test files or new source modules named in the detail body but missing from `files:`. The drift makes the machine-readable manifest unreliable for downstream tooling and review. _(e.g. Steps 2, 3, 5, 7 each cited 3+ tests/test_*.py paths in their detail bodies (e.g., `tests/test_create.py`, `tests/test_progress.py`, `tests/test_run_log.py`) with none of them in step.files.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:run-log-structured-events</sub>
- **Review/detail should not run — or should refuse — on a plan whose major body sections (Overview, Problem statement, Architecture, Risks, Testing, Acceptance criteria, Deferred) are still `_(TODO)_` placeholders; the resulting finding noise drowns out real signals.** — This review's MEDIUM list was dominated by 8 body-completeness findings, each restating the same fact (the plan body is unwritten). Real findings (files-coherence drift across 8 steps) had to compete with this noise. A pre-flight check would have rejected the run. _(e.g. Every one of `## Overview`, `## Problem statement`, `## Architecture`, `## Out of scope`, `## Risks`, `## Testing`, `## Acceptance criteria`, `## Deferred` produced a separate placeholder finding.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:run-log-structured-events</sub>
- **Files-coherence checks should ignore paths containing angle-bracketed placeholders (`<repo>`, `<slug>`, `<plan-slug>`) — those describe runtime artifacts or templated locations, not real repo files, and flagging them as 'missing from step.files' is noise.** — Two findings here flagged `<repo>/.plan-tool/runs/<slug>/in-flight.json` and `<repo>/.plan-tool/runs/<plan-slug>/in-flight.json` as drift, but those paths can never legitimately appear in `files:` — they're parameterized by run, not committed to the tree. _(e.g. Step 3 and step 8 each got a files-coherence finding for an `<repo>/.plan-tool/runs/<...>/in-flight.json` path.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:run-log-structured-events</sub>
- **Heuristic 'already done' matches on short or generic tokens (≤6 chars, or single common words like `Run`) and matches inside markdown headings (`# Foo`) should be down-weighted or suppressed — they false-positive against unrelated code and prose.** — The step-8 finding fired on token `Run` matched against `# Run` in `RUNBOOK.md` — a markdown heading, not a code definition. That kind of signal is indistinguishable from noise and undermines trust in the heuristic's other (sometimes valid) matches. _(e.g. `# Run` in `.agents/skills/plan-tool/RUNBOOK.md` triggered an INFO finding that step 8 'looks already done'.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:run-log-structured-events</sub>

- **If a plan body still has `_(TODO)_` placeholders in core sections (Overview, Problem statement, Architecture, Out of scope, Risks, Testing, Acceptance criteria, Deferred) at review time, treat the plan as ungrounded and refuse to proceed to detail steps until they're filled. These sections set the load-bearing context for every step; detail authored against placeholders produces drift like file-coherence misses.** — Detail steps inherit framing from the plan body. When body sections are placeholders, detail authors invent framing ad hoc, which is exactly when file/path drift and scope creep slip in. This review surfaced 8 placeholder sections alongside 20+ files-coherence findings — not a coincidence. _(e.g. This review: `## Overview`, `## Problem statement`, `## Architecture`, `## Out of scope`, `## Risks`, `## Testing`, `## Acceptance criteria`, `## Deferred` were all `_(TODO)_`, and steps 1–7 each had 1–5 file-coherence findings.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:iterative-correctness-completeness-passes</sub>
- **Before submitting a detail step, grep the detail body for every concrete path (anything matching `\w+/[\w/.-]+\.(py|ts|md|json|yml)`) and reconcile each one against the step's `files:` array. Missing entries from `files:` is the dominant drift mode and creates false-positive review noise.** — Detail prose naturally name-checks files (handlers, tests, sibling docs, prompts) as it explains the change. Step `files:` arrays are often drafted before the detail body and then never re-synced. Review currently flags every prose-mention not in `files:` as MEDIUM; aligning them at authoring time is cheaper than re-litigating at review time. _(e.g. This review: every one of 7 steps had at least one detail-cited path missing from `files:` — `tests/test_cli.py`, `tests/test_create.py`, `tests/test_detail.py`, `prompts/detail-quality.md`, `prompts/check.md`, `prompts/review.md` all appeared in detail bodies but were absent from the corresponding step's `files:` array.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:iterative-correctness-completeness-passes</sub>
- **Test file paths mentioned in a detail body (e.g. `tests/test_X.py`) are almost always real file modifications and belong in the step's `files:` array. Detail authors tend to mention tests as part of "how we verify" prose, then forget that the test file is itself a code change the step is making.** — A specific test file named in prose implies new or modified test code in that file. Omitting it from `files:` understates the step's blast radius, hides the touched surface from grep/audit, and is the single most common files-coherence finding type. _(e.g. This review: `tests/test_cli.py` was cited in steps 4, 6, and 7's detail bodies but absent from each step's `files:`; same pattern for `tests/test_create.py`, `tests/test_detail.py`, `tests/test_convergence.py`, `tests/test_review_walk.py`.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:iterative-correctness-completeness-passes</sub>
- **When a plan's detail mentions a path that uses a project-relative form (`plan_tool/run.py`) and another that uses a skill-anchored form (`.agents/skills/plan-tool/plan_tool/run.py`) for the same file, normalize to one form per plan and stick to it. Mixed forms produce duplicate files-coherence findings against the same file.** — Review compares string-equality against the step's `files:` list. The same file referenced two ways shows up as two distinct "missing" findings, inflating review noise and making it harder to see real omissions. _(e.g. This review flagged `.agents/skills/plan-tool/tests/test_cli.py` (step 5) and `tests/test_cli.py` (steps 4, 6, 7) separately even though they're the same file under different anchoring conventions.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-14 via review:iterative-correctness-completeness-passes</sub>

- **When a step adds a field to a pydantic (or any schema-defining) model, the step's `files:` should include the model's test file alongside the model file — at minimum you owe defaulted-parse, present-value-parse, type-rejection, and round-trip-emission tests at the schema layer that downstream consumer-layer tests won't cover.** — Create commonly proposes only the source file when the change is 'one line in a class'. Detail then discovers the test work has to land either by expanding scope or by hoping a downstream step's tests are sufficient — they rarely are at the schema layer. _(e.g. Step 1 here proposed `files: [schema.py]` only; the detail had to flag that 6 schema-level cases belong in `tests/test_parser.py` (defaulted, present, non-list, non-string-element, round-trip-omits-default, round-trip-preserves-set) that step 4's review-layer tests won't reach.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#1</sub>
- **Before adding an optional list/dict field with `default_factory=...` to a serialized model, grep the writer/serializer for `exclude_defaults` / `exclude_none` and state explicitly in the detail whether the new default will be elided on rewrite — fixtures and on-disk artifacts depend on this.** — A `default_factory=list` that *gets emitted* would silently rewrite every existing fixture/plan on the next save, polluting diffs and breaking 'round-trip with no changes' invariants. A `default_factory=list` that *gets elided* (because of `exclude_defaults=True`) is the safe shape — but you can't tell which one applies without reading the writer. _(e.g. Detail confirmed `parser.write_plan` calls `model_dump(exclude_defaults=True, exclude_none=True)`, so the new `allowed_mentions: []` default won't surface in existing plans — and called this out so reviewers don't expect fixture churn.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#1</sub>
- **When the plan body cites a specific external library as authoritative for behavior, verify the codebase actually imports that library before treating the reference as load-bearing — and flag misattributions in the detail's References section.** — Plans inherit references from create's heuristic, which can name plausible-looking libraries the codebase doesn't actually use. Future steps that trust the citation will design against the wrong semantics. _(e.g. Plan referenced `python-frontmatter` for YAML semantics, but the code uses `yaml.safe_load` / `yaml.safe_dump` directly (parser.py:80,123). Detail noted the misattribution so step 5's docs work doesn't propagate it.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#1</sub>
- **When extending a model that has `ConfigDict(extra='forbid')` (or equivalent strict-input config), the detail must explicitly mark that config as load-bearing and forbid relaxing it as part of the same change.** — Adding a new field while a downstream test relies on typos being rejected creates an obvious-but-wrong shortcut: 'just set extra=ignore and the typo test will also pass with a no-op'. Detail-time guardrails prevent that during step 4. _(e.g. Step 4's planned typo-rejection test (`allowed_mention` singular must still raise) depends on `extra='forbid'` remaining on `Step`. Detail spelled this out so step 4's implementer doesn't loosen the config to 'fix' an unrelated failure.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#1</sub>
- **For 'schema half' steps that exist to gate later consumer steps, the detail's Deferred section should also list shape-decisions that are intentionally NOT enforced here (e.g. cross-field validators, disjointness rules) and explain why — so a later reviewer doesn't ask 'why didn't you add the disjoint-set validator?'** — Schema steps attract preemptive validation rules that conflict with how the consumer will actually use the field. Naming the rejected validator-by-name in the detail saves the next reviewer a full re-litigation. _(e.g. Detail explicitly rejected a 'must be disjoint from files' model_validator on `allowed_mentions`, with rationale tied to step 2's planned union approach (`step.files | step.allowed_mentions`).)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#1</sub>

- **When a step changes a module-level constant (severity, threshold, default), the detail should enumerate every read site of that constant — even when there's only one — so the reviewer can verify scope is genuinely narrow rather than 'looks narrow'.** — Constants in well-factored code often have exactly one read site, but the detail author still has to grep to confirm that. Stating the count and location in the detail turns an implicit claim ('this is a one-line change') into an auditable one, and pre-empts a reviewer asking 'are you sure nothing else reads this?'. _(e.g. Step 2 here flipped `_FILES_COHERENCE_DRIFT_SEV` from 'medium' to 'info' and explicitly noted: 'The constant is read in exactly one place, `_files_coherence_recommendation` at `review.py:1820`.' Without that, the one-line edit looks suspiciously small.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#2</sub>
- **When a step deliberately leaves the codebase in a self-inconsistent intermediate state — e.g. a severity drops to 'info' but a downstream blocking branch still trips on the same condition — the detail must (a) name the inconsistency, (b) point at the step that closes it, and (c) say why doing both here would be wrong. Otherwise the next reviewer flags the inconsistency as a bug and the step's author has to re-litigate scope.** — Multi-step plans routinely sequence semantic changes (this step makes finding info-level) before mechanical changes (next step makes the gate stop blocking on info-level). Without an explicit 'this inconsistency is the signal that step N+1 is still needed' note, reviewers either assume incompleteness or merge the two steps and inflate scope. _(e.g. Step 2's detail called out that after this step alone, `cli.py:4365-4366` still returns 1 on `detail_only` findings even though their severity is now 'info' — and explicitly routed that fix to step 3. The mismatch is intentional and load-bearing for the plan's sequencing.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#2</sub>
- **When a single rendered output (a warning prefix, a JSON severity field, a recommendation string) is emitted by multiple call sites — typically a CLI command path AND an embedded-section render path — the detail should list every renderer it found, even ones deferred to later steps, so the next step's author doesn't fix one and miss the other.** — Plan-tool / CLI codebases frequently have parallel renderers (cmd_X path vs cmd_Y-embeds-X path) that emit identical user-facing strings. They tend to drift if a change touches one and not the other. Surfacing the full list in the detail — even as 'these stay untouched here' — gives step N+1 a complete checklist. _(e.g. Step 2 noted that the rendered `WARN` prefix is emitted by both `_render_completeness_section` at `review.py:1413-1427` AND the inline path in `cmd_completeness` at `cli.py:4288` — and that step 3 must change both together. Listing only one would have set step 3's author up for a half-fix.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#2</sub>
- **When a step adds a field that a sibling step's existing comparison logic must learn to consume, the consumer-step's detail must justify which side(s) of the comparison the new field affects, not treat union-it-in as self-evident. State the chosen direction and why the other direction is wrong.** — An 'endorses X' / 'requires X' field can plausibly union into either side of a set comparison. Picking the wrong side produces semantically backwards behavior (e.g. an opt-in field becoming an obligation). The reasoning is rarely obvious from create's one-line step title, and capturing it in the detail prevents the reviewer or step-N+1 implementer from flipping it. _(e.g. Step 2's detail spelled out why `step.allowed_mentions` unions into the `detail_only` direction but NOT the `files_only` direction: an endorsed *citation* permits a reference, it doesn't demand the file be mentioned. Putting it on both sides would turn an allowance into a requirement and emit a nit for legitimately-quiet endorsed paths.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#2</sub>
- **When test work is deferred to a later step, the current step's Tests section should enumerate the deferred test cases by name with a one-line description of each — not punt with 'see step N'. This becomes the test step's load-bearing checklist and reveals coverage gaps early.** — Without an explicit checklist in the implementing step, the test step's author has to re-derive the full surface area from the code, which usually misses 1-2 corner cases (overlap, no-op defaults, the unchanged-direction regression guard). Listing them by name in the implementer's detail forces enumeration before code lands. _(e.g. Step 2 listed six named tests it expected step 4 to write (endorsement-no-finding, unrecognized-still-flagged, severity-info, severity-unchanged-nit, allowed-mention-not-cited-no-nit, overlap-harmless) — the last three would have been easy to omit if step 4 had only the prose to work from.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#2</sub>

- **When a step's `files:` lists only one file but the file contains multiple independent edit sites for the same semantic change, the detail must enumerate every site with `file:line` refs — create-time audits should count edit *sites*, not just files, since a 'low complexity / 1 file' step with three independent line ranges still risks an implementer missing one.** — Surfacing only the file lets the implementer find the most obvious edit and skip the rest, producing a half-landed semantic change that the next step's tests then expose. The cli.py file here had three independent locations for the same severity demotion (exit-code branch, JSON severity ternary, grouped-report prefix) and missing any one would leave the cli surface internally inconsistent. _(e.g. Step 3 here required edits at `cli.py:4193-4195` (JSON), `cli.py:4288-4292` (grouped-report prefix), and `cli.py:4365-4366` (exit-code branch) — the create proposal `files: [cli.py]` was correct but said nothing about three sites.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#3</sub>
- **When the current step is one slice of a multi-step refactor with shared semantics (e.g. a severity demotion across constants → renderers → tests → docs), the detail must include an 'edits intentionally not made in this step' section with `file:line` refs and an explicit route to the step that owns each deferred edit — and must explicitly resist refactors that are tempting but out of scope.** — Without this, the implementer either silently widens the diff (cross-file parity edit that breaks the next step's frontmatter) or quietly skips a related edit thinking it's out of scope. The detail is the only place that disambiguates 'this step touches X but not Y because Y is step N'. _(e.g. Step 3 here had to explicitly defer the parallel `WARN`→`INFO` edit at `review.py:1419` (in `_render_completeness_section`) because step 3's `files:` was cli.py-only — even though step 2's Deferred section had nominated step 3 for that parity edit.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#3</sub>
- **When a step is part of a multi-step refactor and a *prior* step's Deferred section nominated the current step for a follow-on edit that falls outside the current step's `files:` scope, surface that tension explicitly in the new detail's Deferred section — either request a frontmatter expansion or re-defer with a named reason; never silently widen scope or silently drop the nomination.** — Deferred-section routing is the cross-step contract. If step N defers something to step N+1 and step N+1 silently drops it, the work falls through the cracks and the plan author can't audit it from `git log` of plan edits alone. Surfacing the tension forces a deliberate decision. _(e.g. Step 2's Deferred section nominated step 3 to flip `WARN`→`INFO` in both `cli.py` and `review.py`, but step 3's `files:` was cli.py-only. The detail re-deferred the review.py edit and called out the contract gap rather than expanding the diff.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#3</sub>
- **Before drafting a detail for a step that modifies a function, grep the test corpus for the function's name to determine whether you're modifying tested behavior, adding the first tests, or relying on a later step's test slice. This decision changes whether the current step writes tests, the current step writes a smoke check, or the current step enumerates cases for a later test-only step to cover.** — Without this check, the detail either over-promises ('all existing tests pass' when no tests exist), under-promises ('no test impact' when there are five callers under test), or duplicates work that the test step will redo. The grep is cheap and disambiguates a load-bearing scope question. _(e.g. Step 3 here grepped for `_completeness_exit_code` in the tests dir and confirmed no existing tests — which is why the detail enumerated six test cases as 'work for step 4' rather than writing them inline or claiming a regression-safety guarantee it couldn't make.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#3</sub>
- **When tempted to refactor adjacent code (e.g. 'replace this literal `"info"` with an imported constant from the upstream module'), check whether the *neighbor* lines in the same block use the same style — if they're all literals, preserving local consistency beats global DRY. The detail should explicitly call out and reject the tempting refactor with a 1-line reason, so a reviewer doesn't push back later.** — Single-site refactors-while-editing create stylistic inconsistencies that later readers have to puzzle over ('why is this one constant-driven and the four neighbors aren't?'), and often introduce new cross-module imports that cement a private symbol's reach. Explicitly rejecting the refactor in the detail saves a review cycle. _(e.g. Step 3 here resisted `"severity": _FILES_COHERENCE_DRIFT_SEV if i.kind == "detail_only" else _FILES_COHERENCE_NIT_SEV` and stuck with literal `"info"`, because the four neighbor severities at `cli.py:4163/4173/4183/4204` are also literals — and called out the alternative in the Deferred section.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#3</sub>
- **When a step's only edit to a function deletes the function's last read of one parameter, default to keeping the parameter (with a 1-line note labeling it 'accepted but unused') rather than dropping it from the signature — dropping forces touching every call site for zero functional benefit. The detail should make this call explicit and route 'drop the parameter' to Deferred.** — Parameter-hygiene refactors that touch N call sites are larger blast-radius than they look, and tend to mask the actual semantic change (severity demotion, in this case) under churn. Keeping the parameter preserves a minimal diff and leaves a single deliberate decision in the detail for the next refactor to revisit. _(e.g. Step 3 here kept the `files_coh_issues` keyword on `_completeness_exit_code` after deleting its only read, rather than removing it and touching both call sites at `cli.py:4213` and `cli.py:4328` — and routed the cleanup to Deferred.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#3</sub>

- **Test-writing steps should include 'mutation-style' acceptance criteria — explicit claims of the form 'reverting source edit at file:line causes test X (and only test X) to fail'. This proves pinpoint coverage at review time and prevents tests that pass without actually exercising the contract they claim to pin.** — Without this, a test step can land all green while testing the wrong surface (e.g. asserting on a dataclass field that doesn't carry the property being changed). The revert-to-fail mapping forces each new test back to a specific source edit and surfaces the gap between 'tests pass' and 'tests pin the behavior'. _(e.g. Step 4 here ended its Acceptance Criteria with five 'reverting step N's edit at file:line causes test X to fail' bullets, each tied to a different source-side edit from steps 1-3.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#4</sub>
- **When a test-writing step's `files:` scope names a single test file, the create-time audit should glob for sibling test files (e.g. `test_parser.py` next to `test_review.py`) and call out any test cases whose *natural* home is in a sibling. Either widen scope or have the detail explicitly defer the migration as a chore: follow-up.** — Test files are organized by surface (parser tests vs review tests), but a step that adds schema-rejection tests to a 'review' file may belong in 'parser'. Leaving this implicit means either the test lands in the wrong file or the implementer silently widens scope past the plan's `files:`. _(e.g. Step 4's schema-rejection tests naturally belong in `tests/test_parser.py` next to the existing `TestValidationErrors` class, but the plan scoped `files:` to `tests/test_review.py` only. The detail had to flag this and defer the migration.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#4</sub>
- **Test-step complexity should reflect the number of distinct entry points exercised, not the number of test cases. A 7-test step that touches three entry points (direct function call + cli main + parser) is `medium`; a 7-test step that exercises one entry point through parametrization is `small`.** — Per-entry-point setup (imports, fixtures, capsys vs json-parse, error-class to assert on) dominates the work, not the case count. Counting cases under-budgets review for cross-surface test steps. _(e.g. Step 4's seven tests crossed `check_files_coherence` (direct), `cli_module.main(['completeness'])` (both JSON and grouped paths), and `parse_plan_text` (schema rejection) — justifying medium despite each individual test being ~20 lines.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#4</sub>
- **When a test step adds a negative assertion (e.g. 'typo X is rejected'), the detail should require a paired positive control (e.g. 'corrected form Y is accepted'). Without the control, the negative test can pass for the wrong reason — both the typo and the corrected form being rejected, or the entire validation chain failing to load.** — Negative-only assertions are silently satisfied by 'everything fails' bugs. The positive control forces the chain to be wired end-to-end and pins which side of the boundary the change moved. _(e.g. Step 4's `test_singular_allowed_mention_field_rejected` is paired with `test_allowed_mentions_plural_list_accepted` precisely so a regression that drops the plural field too is caught.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#4</sub>
- **Test-step details should explicitly call out any existing test that must remain byte-identical as a 'control', citing it by file:line and forbidding edits. Otherwise implementers will 'improve' a test that was load-bearing as a contrast case.** — Test refactors look harmless but a test pair (drift vs no-drift, endorsed vs unendorsed) only works as a regression guard if both halves stay aligned. Calling out the control by file:line makes the implicit pair explicit. _(e.g. Step 4's detail explicitly required `TestCheckFilesCoherence.test_detail_only_drift_flagged` at `tests/test_review.py:2792` to remain byte-identical so it continues to act as a control against `test_allowed_mentions_endorses_cited_path`.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#4</sub>

- **Doc-publication steps that close the loop on a multi-step feature must depend on every step that lands behavior the docs describe, and must explicitly re-state changed exit-code / severity semantics in user-facing prose.** — Quoting a new severity before the demote step lands documents behavior the code doesn't have. CI operators relying on the old failure semantics need the doc to actively contradict the prior wording, not just omit it. _(e.g. Step 5 here documented info-level files-coherence findings that step 2 demoted from medium and step 3 stopped exiting non-zero on; the bullet had to add 'Neither severity fails plan-tool completeness — both are advisory' explicitly so a CI gate writer wouldn't carry over the medium-era assumption.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#5</sub>
- **When a doc edit changes the meaning of an existing sentence (not just adds new content), grep for the exact old wording across all docs and add a zero-hit acceptance criterion. Replacement-edits silently leave stale duplicates elsewhere otherwise.** — Multiple user-facing docs tend to paraphrase the same load-bearing sentence. Editing one and not the others creates contradictory documentation that's harder to debug than missing documentation. _(e.g. Acceptance criterion `grep -n 'medium when detail cites' README.md returns zero hits` after rewriting the Files coherence bullet — guarantees the old wording isn't preserved in a sibling doc the editor missed.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#5</sub>
- **Coin a memorable, distinctive phrase for any new conceptual distinction the feature introduces, and use it verbatim across every doc that touches the concept. The phrase becomes the future grep anchor.** — Cross-doc questions like 'where do I read about X?' rely on grep, not navigation. A phrase that appears in PLAN_FORMAT and RUNBOOK and SKILL with identical spelling lets a future agent or human land in any of them. _(e.g. 'explanatory-reference vs edit-target distinction' was used verbatim in the field-semantics paragraph (PLAN_FORMAT.md) and the runbook subsection prose, so a grep for either side of the distinction lands on both surfaces.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#5</sub>
- **When a step adds a feature to a meta-tool (lint/test/plan tool), use the step's own frontmatter / metadata to consume the new feature as a dogfood test — and call this out explicitly in the step's acceptance criteria.** — Self-application is the cheapest possible end-to-end test. It also makes the step's diff serve as a worked example for readers, with no separate fixture to maintain. _(e.g. Step 5 added `allowed_mentions:` to its own frontmatter (citing the schema/review/cli/test files it references in prose) so running `plan-tool completeness` against the plan itself verifies the new field works for a real cross-step citation.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#5</sub>
- **Doc-publication steps should add NEW rows to existing decision-tree / quick-reference tables rather than expanding agent-facing frontmatter `description` strings. Tables grow cleanly; frontmatter has a readability budget.** — Agent-catalog descriptions are loaded into routing prompts; pushing them past ~1500 chars degrades discovery for every other intent without making the new feature easier to find. _(e.g. SKILL.md decision-tree got a new row routing 'completeness flagged a path my detail cites but doesn't edit' to `allowed_mentions:`; the frontmatter description was deliberately left byte-identical.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#5</sub>
- **The 'Deferred' section of a doc-publication step should enumerate plausible-but-out-of-scope alternative doc surfaces (prompt edits, auto-population, migration sweeps, strict-mode CI gates) so the next planner doesn't propose them as fresh work.** — Doc steps look superficially mechanical, so reviewers and follow-up planners frequently propose 'while we're at it' adjacent surfaces. Pre-routing those to a follow-up plan keeps scope tight and surfaces the explicit decision not to bundle. _(e.g. Step 5's Deferred listed prompts/detail.md auto-population, comment-marker suppression, migration sweeps over existing plans, and a --strict-completeness CI flag — each routed to a separate future plan.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:structural-finding-recognition#5</sub>

- **When the already-implemented signal matches a generic symbol name (e.g. `parser`, `handler`, `client`), the detail must grep for the *specific* new symbols proposed by the step's title before relying on the signal — generic-name matches are usually false positives.** — The heuristic flagged `def parser` / `class Parser` matches in both target files, but neither was Decision-related. A `Decision`/`parse_decision`/`Direction` grep returning empty was the load-bearing confirmation, not the heuristic hit. _(e.g. Step 1 here: heuristic said review.py + test_review.py were partially implemented; actual grep for `Decision|parse_decision_block|Direction` returned zero.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#1</sub>
- **When a step's title explicitly names a backward-compat concern (e.g. "tolerates absent block for backward compat"), the detail should promote that concern to its own acceptance criterion AND its own test method — not bury it as one bullet inside a general parser test.** — Backward-compat is the single most likely thing a downstream step (gate, rollup) will silently break. Naming it discretely in acceptance criteria forces step 3+ to verify it survived rather than discovering regressions during integration. _(e.g. Step 1's `test_returns_none_when_heading_absent` is a dedicated test, and acceptance criteria call out `parse_decision_block("")` and "no heading present" as separate verifiable contracts.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#1</sub>
- **For parser/dataclass-introducing steps whose output will be consumed by multiple later steps, the detail's acceptance criteria should freeze the *contract* (return-type semantics, None vs empty semantics, enum membership) verbatim — not just "the parser works" — so each downstream step can be reviewed against a stable interface.** — Contract drift between step N and step N+2 is the most expensive bug class in a multi-step plan. A frozen contract in acceptance criteria lets later steps be implemented without re-reading step 1's source. _(e.g. Step 1's acceptance criteria pin: "exactly four enum members", "exactly five fields", "None vs Direction.UNKNOWN have distinct meanings" — each becomes a checkable invariant for steps 3–5.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#1</sub>
- **When introducing a new parser into a module that already exposes one, the detail must explicitly justify *not* extending the existing parser, by naming the downstream call sites that would have to change either way.** — Reviewers default to "why not just add this to the existing function?" — answering that question proactively ("the existing parser serves N other prompts that will never emit this block") prevents a round of plan churn. _(e.g. Step 1 explained why `parse_decision_block` lives in review.py rather than folding into launcher.py's `parse_finding_bullets`: launcher serves non-reconcile prompts that never emit Decision blocks.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#1</sub>
- **When adding a test class that's conceptually sibling to an existing one, the detail should cite the existing class by name AND line range, and direct placement adjacent to it — not just "add tests to test_X.py".** — Test files grow long; ad-hoc placement scatters related tests. Co-locating sibling test classes pays off when a future refactor moves both together. _(e.g. Step 1 placed `TestParseDecisionBlock` next to `TestParseFindingBullets` at `tests/test_review.py:2258-2298`.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#1</sub>

- **When step N produces a wire format that another step parses (regex, schema, token list), the detail for N must cite the exact field labels, token spellings, and regex anchors from the consumer side — case-sensitive and punctuation-sensitive — not paraphrase them.** — A single character drift (e.g. dropping `?` from `Fulfills intent?`, pluralizing `Direction` to `Directions`, or indenting a heading so `^##` no longer matches under MULTILINE) silently breaks parsing on every output. The producer step's detail is the last chance to lock the contract before claude generates against it. _(e.g. Step 2 here had to repeat verbatim the five labels (`Intent`, `Code state`, `Fulfills intent?`, `Direction`, `Justification`), the four direction tokens (`update-plan`/`update-code`/`no-op`/`unknown`), and the six justification tokens that step 1's `_DECISION_*_RE` constants and `_DIRECTION_TOKENS` set are compiled against.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#2</sub>
- **When a step inverts a default behavior (changes a prompt rule from 'do X' to 'do Y instead'), the detail must name the old behavior, name the new behavior, and explain *why* the inversion is load-bearing — not just describe the new rule.** — Reviewers and future implementers scanning the diff see only the new text and miss that a default flipped. The whole point of an inversion is often the bug being killed; if the detail just describes the new state, the reason is lost and the next rewrite may quietly re-invert it. _(e.g. Step 2's rewrite of rule 3 flipped reconcile's default from 'narrow the plan to match the code' (silent plan-rewrite bug) to 'flag the code as incomplete via status-flip'. The detail called this out explicitly as 'the load-bearing change — it inverts today's default' rather than just describing the new wording.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#2</sub>
- **Multi-step rollouts where a producer step lands before a consumer step's parser refactor create a temporary breakage window. The producer step's detail must call this out in Deferred so the next implementer knows the noisy intermediate commit is expected, not a regression.** — Otherwise the next implementer (or a code reviewer) sees inflated counts / broken output between commits, assumes step 2 was buggy, and either reverts or scope-creeps step 2 to 'fix' something step 3 already owns. _(e.g. Step 2 here adds nested `- **Intent**: ...` lines under `## Findings`, which inflate `parse_finding_bullets`' bullet count until step 3 refactors the slicer. Deferred entry: 'Until step 3 refactors the slicing logic, the bullet count … will be inflated for reconcile runs. Step 3, not step 2, fixes this.')_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#2</sub>
- **When an allow-list is enforced in code (a parser/gate) and also constrains an LLM's output (a prompt), the prompt is the canonical source claude reads — detail the allow-list inline in the prompt, and have the code-side gate cite the prompt rather than the reverse.** — Claude only sees the prompt at runtime; if the canonical list lives in code, the prompt drifts. The gate's job is to refuse out-of-list values, but it only fires when claude already produced one — the prompt is where claude actually picks the token. _(e.g. Step 2's `## Decision block per finding` section enumerates the six justification tokens inline in `reconcile.md`; step 3's `launcher.apply_finding` gate 'encodes the same set' and points back to the prompt as canonical, not vice versa.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#2</sub>
- **Detail for steps that edit prompts must enumerate the template interpolation surface (`${step_detail}`, `${lessons_section}`, etc.) as load-bearing and forbid touching it, even when rewriting the surrounding section.** — Prompt-builder placeholders are easy to break with an inattentive rewrite (an extra blank line, a removed trailing newline, an accidental re-indent) and the failure mode is silent — claude just sees a missing context block. _(e.g. Step 2's detail explicitly preserved the `_(none)_` empty-block convention and called out '`${lessons_section}` placeholder … Leave at end-of-file untouched. This is interpolated by the prompt builder and is orthogonal to the Decision-block contract.')_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#2</sub>

- **When step N-1's Deferred section names a refactor as a prerequisite for step N (e.g. "parser must be widened before the gate is meaningful"), step N's create proposal should bump complexity and add the prerequisite file to its `files` list — detail should treat the prior step's Deferred section as load-bearing input, not optional context.** — If detail discovers the prerequisite mid-draft, the step's stated scope is wrong and the complexity rating under-budgets review. Here, step 2's deferred parser refactor was the single biggest piece of work in step 3 and wasn't visible from step 3's title. _(e.g. Step 3 here had to refactor `parse_finding_bullets` (~40 lines) AND parallel-fix `summarize_findings` before the new gate would even fire — both invisible from the proposed title "thread Decision into apply_finding".)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#3</sub>
- **When a step adds a new verdict/enum value, the create-time audit should grep for every switch/dispatch on that enum and either include those files in scope or explicitly defer with a one-line note on the interim user-visible behavior.** — New enum values silently fall through dispatchers to a default branch, producing wrong-but-not-broken output. Surfacing the dispatcher list at create time lets the author decide consciously whether to widen scope or accept the interim. _(e.g. Adding `REFUSED` to `ApplyFindingResult.verdict` required deciding whether to update `_dispatch_apply_finding` in `cli.py:3207-3352` (declined; routed to step 5) — a decision the create-time proposal didn't surface.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#3</sub>
- **When a vocabulary or allow-list will live in multiple file types (prompt template + source code + docs), the first step that introduces it should pick one canonical home and the detail should explicitly route consolidation work to the docs/cleanup step. Three copies that drift silently break the contract.** — Prompt-side and code-side enumerations of the same set will diverge under maintenance pressure; flagging the duplication during the introducing step's detail is the cheapest moment to record the drift hazard. _(e.g. Six `Justification` tokens live in `prompts/reconcile.md`, `prompts/apply-finding.md`, and `_ALLOWED_PLAN_UPDATE_JUSTIFICATIONS` in `launcher.py`. The detail's Deferred section routed consolidation (or a CI consistency check) to the documentation step.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#3</sub>
- **When a step changes the body shape of a parsed structure (multi-line vs single-line, embedded sub-blocks, etc.), the detail should enumerate every consumer of that body field and verify their tolerance — `body.splitlines()[0]`-style defenses are common but not guaranteed.** — Body-shape changes are silent contract breaks; downstream code that assumed single-line will truncate, mis-key, or crash. The audit is mechanical (grep for `.body`) but easy to skip when you're focused on the producer side. _(e.g. After the slicer refactor, `FindingBullet.body` became multi-line. The detail confirmed `extract_reconcile_recommendations` (`review.py:2058`) and `_claude_recommendations` (`review.py:2083`) both already do `body.splitlines()[0][:120]` — defensive by accident, not by contract.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#3</sub>
- **For gate / refusal steps, the detail's tests section should explicitly include a backward-compat assertion that legacy inputs (no gate-trigger structure present) still take the no-op path — and confirm the existing test suite doesn't need new setup to keep passing.** — Gates are easy to over-fire. Without an explicit "None-decision falls through" test plus a survey of pre-existing tests' fixture shape, a single regex tweak can flip dozens of unrelated tests from accept to refuse. _(e.g. The detail confirmed every test in `TestApplyFindingHappyPath`, `TestApplyFindingFailureHandling`, `TestApplyFindingValidationDelta`, and `TestApplyFindingAutoFixIntroduced` (`:2164-2851`) uses bodies without `## Decision` blocks, so the gate's None-branch preserves their behavior with no fixture edits.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#3</sub>

- **"Add a CLI flag" steps systematically under-rate complexity because the flag is one argparse line but threading it through every reaching dispatch path is the actual work. Complexity ratings should count downstream call sites, not just the argparse + one consumer.** — This step was rated `low` with 2 files; in practice it touched 7 call sites across 3 files and added ~11 test methods. The work scales with how many helpers sit between argparse and the consuming function, not with the flag declaration. _(e.g. Step 4 here threaded `args.strict_intent` through `_build_review_action_fn` (1 signature widening + 5 caller updates), `_dispatch_apply_finding` (the actual `apply_finding` kwarg), and `cmd_completeness` (a parallel direct-call path). The argparse declaration was one block; the threading was the bulk of the diff.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#4</sub>
- **When step N's Deferred section names a specific signature/hook that step N+1 will rely on (e.g. "step 4 will add `allowed_justifications` kwarg to apply_finding"), step N+1's declared `files:` list MUST include the file that owns that hook, even when N+1's nominal scope is elsewhere.** — Create-time heuristics treat each step in isolation and miss cross-step deferrals. The reader of N+1 then has to re-read N's deferred section mid-draft to discover the load-bearing edit, and the step's complexity rating is wrong. _(e.g. Step 3's detail at `03-...md:241,:427` deferred widening `apply_finding(... allowed_justifications=...)` to step 4. Step 4's `files:` listed only `cli.py` and `test_cli.py` — but landing the flag was impossible without also touching `launcher.py`. Create should have read step 3's Deferred section and propagated the launcher edit forward.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#4</sub>
- **When a step adds a kwarg that overrides a module-level constant, the safe Python pattern is `Optional[T] = None` (sentinel resolved at call time), NOT `T = MODULE_CONSTANT` as the default. The latter freezes identity at import time and silently shadows future revisions of the constant.** — This is a subtle correctness issue that won't fail tests today but will misbehave the day someone reassigns the constant. Detail-drafting should default to the sentinel pattern without re-litigating it each time. _(e.g. Step 4's `apply_finding(..., allowed_justifications: Optional[frozenset[str]] = None)` resolves to `_ALLOWED_PLAN_UPDATE_JUSTIFICATIONS` inside the function body; if it had defaulted to the constant directly, a later edit to the constant module-side would not be reflected in callers that don't pass the kwarg.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#4</sub>
- **For "CLI flag flips downstream behavior" steps, the test plan must include BOTH plumbing tests (kwarg arrives at the consumer with the right value) AND short-circuit tests (the consumer's behavior actually changes). Either alone is insufficient — a regression that threads the kwarg but skips applying it inside the consumer would pass plumbing tests silently.** — It's tempting to write only plumbing tests because they're easier to set up (monkey-patch the consumer, record kwargs). But that leaves a class of regressions undetected. Detail-drafting should always pair them. _(e.g. Step 4's `TestStrictIntentPlumbing` records `allowed_justifications` on a monkey-patched `apply_finding`; `TestStrictIntentShortCircuit` runs the real `apply_finding` and asserts the invoker was NOT called. The second class catches a class of bug the first can't see.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#4</sub>

- **For 'emit a summary / observability' steps, split the work into a pure formatter (data → string) and a data-builder (parses state + joins side-effect-collected info) so each gets a clean unit-test surface; create's `files: [src, test]` tuple under-counts the helper surface area.** — A single combined helper forces tests to stand up real parsed bodies just to check formatting, and forces formatter changes to re-mock the data side. The split lets the formatter take synthetic dataclass entries and the data-builder be tested against real parser output. _(e.g. Step 5 here split into `_format_decision_rollup(iteration, entries) -> str` (pure) and `_build_decision_log_entries(recs, refused_ids) -> list[DecisionRollupEntry]` (calls `parse_decision_block`). Tests use synthetic `DecisionRollupEntry` lists for the formatter and synthetic `Recommendation` bodies for the builder — no need to drive the full walk for either.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#5</sub>
- **When the step's new code references symbols, literals, or verdict strings introduced multiple steps earlier than its declared `depends_on`, the detail's 'Why' section must enumerate those transitive dependencies and name the step that introduces each. `depends_on: N` alone is misleading when steps < N are also load-bearing.** — Create only proposes the immediate prior step. Reviewers and downstream implementers reading `depends_on: 3` assume steps 1–2 are decorative; if step 5 actually needs the `Direction` enum from step 1 and the `REFUSED` literal from step 3, that needs to be explicit or rebases that drop step 1 will silently break step 5. _(e.g. Step 5's detail had `depends_on: 3` but also needed `Direction` and `parse_decision_block` (step 1) plus the `REFUSED` verdict literal (step 3); the 'Why' section spelled out both dependencies and which step introduces each.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#5</sub>
- **When adding an optional kwarg to a shared helper used at multiple call sites, the detail must (a) enumerate every existing call site, (b) state which sites opt in vs stay unchanged with `=None`, and (c) include a regression-guard acceptance criterion that the unchanged sites still produce identical output.** — Optional kwargs make 'action at a distance' bugs trivial to introduce — a call site you forgot about silently gains the new behaviour, or worse, opts in by accident because someone passes `**kwargs` through. Listing the sites + an explicit no-change criterion forces the author to look at each one. _(e.g. Step 5 added `decision_log: Optional[set[str]] = None` to `_build_review_action_fn`; the detail listed `_save_and_walk_reconcile`, `_iterate_reconcile_loop`, `cmd_review`, `cmd_review_walk`, and the recursive `_dispatch_reconcile` call, with one acceptance criterion that the three unchanged sites produce no rollup heading in their stdout (regression guard).)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#5</sub>
- **When the modified feature has a recursion / nested-dispatch path, the detail must explicitly decide whether the new behaviour fires inside the recursion and state the decision — don't leave it implicit. Defaulting to 'off' is usually right but should be stated, not inferred from omission.** — Without an explicit decision, the next implementer touching the recursion site re-litigates the question or worse, accidentally inverts the choice. Naming the recursion site + the decision documents the UX trade-off (e.g. nested rollups would be hard to read) for future readers. _(e.g. Step 5's `_dispatch_reconcile` recursion at `cli.py:3164-3172` constructs a fresh action_fn for nested sub-walks; the detail explicitly chose to leave `decision_log=None` there (no nested rollup) and tracked the 'should nested calls indent / suppress / show their own rollup?' question under Deferred.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#5</sub>

- **When a doc-only step documents a gate/flag introduced upstream, the detail must identify *all* sources of truth for the relevant tokens (constants in code, inlined lists in prompts, tables in docs) and require byte-identical sync across them — and create-time should mark these steps medium, not low.** — Three-way token sync is the load-bearing risk of these doc steps; a 'low' rating under-budgets review and lets the table drift from the constant silently. _(e.g. Step 6 had to keep six allow-list tokens identical across `_ALLOWED_PLAN_UPDATE_JUSTIFICATIONS` (launcher.py), `prompts/reconcile.md`, and the README table — and that constraint is the single most reviewable acceptance criterion.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#6</sub>
- **For doc steps that introduce a new CLI flag, the detail must enumerate `effective on <subcommand list>` and `no-op on <subcommand list>` explicitly — operators read flag docs to decide where to add the flag, not just what it does.** — A flag that's a global root parser flag but only meaningful on a subset of subcommands is the most common doc gap; without the enumeration, users will pass the flag to read-only verbs and assume it changed something. _(e.g. `--strict-intent` is a root flag but only affects `run --mode reconcile`, `review`, `review-walk` (anywhere `apply_finding` dispatches); a no-op on `validate`/`list`/`status`/`verify`/`review-all`.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#6</sub>
- **Doc-only steps deserve grep-based verification commands as the 'Tests' section — every claim in the doc should map to a concrete `grep -n` that returns a known hit count, since file-inspection is the actual gate.** — Without grep-checkable acceptance, doc steps get reviewed by 'reads ok' which catches typos but not structural omissions (missing cross-references, missing rows in tables, wrong anchor slugs). _(e.g. `grep -n "strict-intent" SKILL.md` must return ≥4 hits (description, argument-hint, new H3, decision-tree row); a result of 3 means one surface was forgotten.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#6</sub>
- **When a doc step spans multiple files with cross-references, fix the authoring order in the Implementation section (e.g. README → RUNBOOK → SKILL) so each later file can cite stable anchors in earlier ones.** — Cross-references authored top-down break less often than ones authored ad-hoc; reviewers can verify anchors in the order the writer wrote them. _(e.g. RUNBOOK's recovery entry links `README.md#intent-preservation-in-reconcile`; SKILL's H3 links both — authoring README last would force re-editing the other two.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#6</sub>
- **When a step documents a new prompt-emitted output schema (a block claude is required to emit, like `## Decision`), explicitly disambiguate it from frontmatter/YAML schema in the Files section so the plan-format doc isn't pulled into scope.** — Reviewers will instinctively flag 'why isn't PLAN_FORMAT.md updated?' when any structured artifact is documented; pre-empting that with a one-line scope note saves a review cycle. _(e.g. Step 6's Files section explicitly noted `PLAN_FORMAT.md` is not in scope because `## Decision` is a prompt artifact, not a frontmatter field.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#6</sub>
- **GitHub-flavored markdown auto-anchor slugs (from H2/H3 titles) are deterministic and verifiable — the Tests section of doc steps should include the slug-rendering check, not just 'anchor exists'.** — `(#the-decision-rollup)` resolves from `## The Decision rollup`; subtle title edits (adding 'a', changing capitalization of a non-leading word) silently break the link. The slug rule is the same everywhere — call it out once per doc step. _(e.g. Acceptance criterion: every `(#...)` cross-reference in this step resolves under GFM slugifier rules — verifiable without rendering.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-15 via detail:intent-preserving-reconcile#6</sub>

- **When a step adds public symbols (functions, dataclasses, parsers) to a module that has a sibling `tests/test_<module>.py`, the test file is a virtually-guaranteed co-edit and create should list it under `files` even if the step body doesn't mention testing.** — Detail-drafting always lands tests for new primitives, and missing the test file from `files` makes the step look smaller than it is — under-budgeting complexity and hiding ~20+ test methods of work. _(e.g. Step 1 here proposed `[lessons.py, lessons-equivalence.md]` but the actual implementation needed three edits — the third being `tests/test_lessons.py` to add `TestParseEquivalenceResponse`, `TestEquivalencePromptRender`, and `TestFindLessonEquivalences` (~26 new test methods).)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#1</sub>
- **Recognize the 'shared primitive + N consumer steps' plan shape: step 1 lands a contract (types + function signature + parser + prompt + tests) and **explicitly does not wire up any caller**. Acceptance criteria should pin every consumed surface so steps 2..N can be reviewed independently.** — Without this discipline, primitive-step PRs accidentally drag in a half-wired caller, or downstream steps duplicate the prompt/parser because the contract was never frozen. Naming the shape unblocks parallel review. _(e.g. Step 1 here ships `find_lesson_equivalences` for two future consumers (`lessons consolidate` CLI in step 2 and `strict_check` on `append_lessons` in step 3); the acceptance criteria explicitly list every importable symbol so step 2 and step 3 plug into a frozen surface.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#1</sub>
- **When a step changes a foundational claim in a module's top docstring (`'no CLI'`, `'no shell-out'`, `'read-only'`, etc.), the docstring update is a required edit, not a courtesy. Detail should call it out as a discrete sub-bullet in Implementation, not bury it in prose.** — Future readers (and grep) treat top-of-module docstrings as authoritative. Leaving a contradicted claim in place erodes trust in every other docstring in the codebase. _(e.g. `plan_tool/lessons.py`'s docstring stated 'no claude shell-out' — step 1 here had to revise that sentence because the new primitive is the first shell-out from the module. The detail flagged it as edit #1 under Implementation rather than letting it slip.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#1</sub>
- **Input-hash / cache-key design for memoized model calls: include only the data the model sees plus anything that defines *result identity*. Exclude post-filter knobs (thresholds, confidence floors, top-K limits) and the prompt template body itself.** — Threshold-change ≠ cache-invalidating — reusing a cached response under a tighter filter is correct and saves tokens. Prompt-template edits are intentional and rare, and dragging the template into the hash would needlessly invalidate every cached call on prompt cleanup. _(e.g. Step 1's `_equivalence_input_hash` hashes only `(rule, why, example)` tuples for existing+candidates; `min_confidence` is applied to results post-return, and the prompt template body isn't read into the hash — matching the established design of `_initial_steps_input_hash` in `create.py`.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#1</sub>

- **For CLI-extension steps that add a new subcommand, the detail must cite the closest existing subcommand as a structural template — covering nested-subparser shape vs flat-verb, default-mutation posture (diff/dry-run vs apply), and TTY-gate behavior. Without an explicit precedent citation, the implementer reinvents shape and drifts from the family.** — CLI families accrete conventions that exist nowhere except in sibling code (where the dispatch table groups verbs, how -y interacts with --apply, whether 'verb action' is nested or flat). Discovering them mid-draft costs reads and produces inconsistent surfaces. _(e.g. Step 2 cited p_checkpoint / p_runs for the nested-subparsers shape and cmd_split for diff-by-default + Y/N confirm; without these anchors the consolidate verb would have landed as a flat verb with no confirmation gate.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#2</sub>
- **When a step's depends_on is non-empty, the detail must restate the prior step's primitive contract (signature, indexing convention, return shape, resume / side-effect surface) inline — not just link to it. A reviewer of step N likely doesn't have step (N-1)'s detail loaded.** — Cross-step contract drift is one of the most expensive review failure modes: the next step silently relies on a behavior the prior step didn't actually guarantee, and the mismatch surfaces only at integration time. _(e.g. Step 2 had to spell out that find_lesson_equivalences uses 1-based indices, accepts candidates=() for intra-set comparison, and defaults to resume-from-checkpoint — all from step 1's contract — so the rewrite's drop-right_index logic is unambiguous.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#2</sub>
- **For maintenance / auto-clean verbs that find both clear-cut and ambiguous categories (e.g. duplicates vs conflicts, dead code vs probably-dead code), the apply path should auto-handle only the clear-cut category and report the ambiguous category in a summary for manual review. Default to diff-by-default + apply-behind-confirm; never auto-merge.** — Ambiguous-category auto-handling causes silent nuance loss and is the cited reason 'no auto-merge' decisions appear in plan risk sections. Future maintenance-verb plans should enumerate explicitly which finding categories drive a mutation and which only annotate. _(e.g. lessons consolidate auto-drops duplicates' right_index but never auto-removes either side of a conflict pair — conflicts surface in the structured summary for manual edit only.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#2</sub>
- **For CLI verbs that wrap a typed primitive with knobs (confidence threshold, resume / no-resume, retry, scope filter), expose those knobs as CLI flags even when sensible defaults exist — operator visibility is cheap and matters most when the verb is misbehaving.** — Operators debugging an unexpected finding need a way to lower the threshold or force a re-judge without editing source. Burying the knob inside the primitive forces a code change for what should be a flag flip. _(e.g. lessons consolidate exposes --min-confidence and --no-resume even though find_lesson_equivalences already has defaults, because the diagnostic value of surfacing borderline cases outweighs the flag-surface cost.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#2</sub>

- **Treat 'symbol exists' from the already-implemented heuristic as a weak signal — confirm the step's specific new contract (new params, new return shape, new behavior), not just the function's presence.** — Heuristic symbol matches catch 'function exists' but miss 'function lacks the new param the step is supposed to add', causing detail writers to either understate the work or over-defer to 'already done' guesses. _(e.g. Here `def append_lessons` matched the heuristic, but the step's actual deliverable was a new `strict_check` keyword-only param plus an `AppendLessonsResult` dataclass — neither existed; the symbol match was misleading.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#3</sub>
- **When step N produces data that step N+1 will consume, name the integration seam concretely in the detail (specific field name, file path, message shape) rather than saying 'step N+1 will pick this up'.** — Without a named seam, step N+1's detail re-litigates the data contract and step N may ship a too-narrow shape that forces step N+1 to widen retroactively. _(e.g. Step 3's detail named `AppendLessonsResult.general_dropped` / `project_dropped` as the explicit seam step 4 will read for its `.plan-tool/lessons-warnings.jsonl` sidecar — locking the contract before step 4 starts.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#3</sub>
- **When two steps would each legitimately add the same private helper because their `depends_on` graphs don't link them, route consolidation explicitly in Deferred — name which helper survives, where it lives, and who deletes the other.** — Implicit 'someone will dedupe later' rots; an explicit routing decision keeps both steps shippable independently while reducing the cleanup to a one-line follow-up rather than a multi-step refactor. _(e.g. Step 3's `_parse_appender_bullets` duplicates regex work that step 2's `parse_existing_entries` lands; Deferred routes consolidation to the step-2 helper (it returns `list[LessonEntry]`, the more natural shape) once both have shipped.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#3</sub>
- **When a step's tests are owned by a later test-landing step, the implementation step's Tests section should still enumerate expected test names as a forward-acceptance contract.** — Plans that defer all tests to one step risk landing implementation steps that quietly disagree about scope; named tests in the producing step's detail give reviewers a concrete grep target after the test step lands. _(e.g. Step 3 deferred test landing to step 5 but still enumerated 13 expected names (e.g. `test_append_strict_check_runs_after_substring_dedupe`, `test_parse_appender_bullets_round_trips_lesson_entry_render`) so step 5's review can confirm coverage by grep.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#3</sub>

- **Audit-trail / sidecar writes (warning logs, run logs, telemetry) must be best-effort: catch all exceptions, emit one stderr notice on failure, and return without raising. The parent operation must never fail because a sidecar disk write failed.** — Sidecars are observability surfaces, not contracts. A read-only filesystem, full disk, or permission flap in `.plan-tool/` should never break `append_lessons` or the parent command — otherwise observability tooling becomes a liability instead of an asset. _(e.g. Step 4's `append_warning(repo_root, record)` wraps its append+trim cycle in one `try/except Exception` and only prints `plan-tool: append_warning failed: …` on stderr; `_append_to_file` calls it unconditionally without checking a return value.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#4</sub>
- **When step N introduces a pass-through hook with a no-op default (e.g. `weight_fn: Callable | None = None`, `recurrence_weight_fn=lambda _f: 1.0`) and step N+1 activates it, the activation step's `files:` must list both the helper's host file AND every call site whose signature widens to pass the hook through.** — Create-time `files:` often lists only the file where the hook fires (e.g. `lessons.py`) and misses the helper signature widening (e.g. `cli._print_consolidate_summary`). The detail then has to rediscover the call-site touch surface mid-draft, which is expensive and easy to half-do. _(e.g. Step 4 activated step 2's `recurrence_weight_fn` hook. Beyond the writer in `lessons.py`, the step had to widen `_print_consolidate_summary` in `cli.py` to accept and apply `weight_fn` per file — a touch the create proposal would have missed.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#4</sub>
- **Adding a CLI flag almost always touches three places, not one: the `add_argument` call, the subparser/CLI help string, and the module-level docstring's command summary. Plans that list only the parser file under-specify the touch surface.** — Help text and module-docstring summaries are part of the user-visible CLI contract — reviewers will flag a flag that doesn't appear in `--help` output AND in the docstring's command table. Catching this at create time avoids a 'docs follow-up' commit. _(e.g. Step 4's `--ignore-recurrence` flag on `p_lessons_consolidate` required (a) the `add_argument` call, (b) help text, AND (c) updating the cli.py module docstring's `lessons consolidate` line to mention recurrence-aware ordering.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#4</sub>
- **When a plan body distinguishes two behavioral paths (e.g. warn-and-continue vs strict-drop, dry-run vs apply), the detail must verify that the param/branch for each path actually exists in the codebase yet. A previous step may have wired only one path, leaving the other path's param entirely absent.** — Create proposals tend to describe the goal state ('add the writer to both paths') without checking whether both paths are reachable. If the second path's gating param doesn't exist, the step's scope silently expands to 'add the param AND the writer'. _(e.g. Step 3 introduced `strict_check` only; step 4's plan said 'add writer in warn-and-continue and strict-drop paths' but the warn-and-continue branch had no `warn_check` param yet. Step 4 had to add the param itself before threading the writer.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#4</sub>
- **JSONL log records (or any append-only on-disk record format) should ship with defensive readers — unknown fields ignored, missing fields defaulted via explicit `.get(..., default)` — so the schema can evolve additively without a version field or a migration step.** — A `dataclasses.asdict` / `dataclass(**obj)` round-trip is brittle: adding one optional field on the dataclass breaks every existing on-disk line. Explicit `from_json` getters with defaults give you forward compat for free, and a `schema_version` field can be deferred until the first non-additive change. _(e.g. Step 4's `LessonsWarning.from_json` uses `obj.get('matched_rules', []) or ()` and friends instead of `cls(**obj)`, so future additive fields don't require a versioning bump.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#4</sub>
- **Silent cap/rotation/trim operations are debugging hell. When a rolling cap fires, when a checkpoint expires, when a cache evicts — emit one short stderr line so the cleanup is observable. Never silently shrink user-visible state.** — A reviewer running `plan-tool lessons consolidate` and seeing fewer warning records than expected has no signal to distinguish 'nothing was ever logged' from 'logs were trimmed under cap'. One line on stderr per trim event closes that gap without adding a real logging dependency. _(e.g. Step 4's `append_warning` emits `plan-tool: lessons-warnings.jsonl trimmed to N records (was M)` on stderr whenever the rolling cap fires; the test fixture asserts the line is present after a boundary-crossing append.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#4</sub>
- **The 'already-implemented signal' from the create heuristic is misleading when the step's actual job is to *modify* the matched function (widen a signature, add a param, extend a branch). The signal needs to distinguish 'this step introduces fn X' from 'this step modifies fn X' before reviewers can use it.** — Treating the matched function as 'done' under-budgets the step. The detail-drafter has to read the existing function fully to understand which additions are net-new vs which existing branches they extend — but the create proposal frames it as low-risk because the function exists. _(e.g. Step 4's heuristic flagged `append_lessons` as possibly-already-implemented (the function does exist). But step 4 widens its signature (`warn_check` param), adds two new result fields, and rewrites the strict-drop branch's tail. The signal needed a 'modify, not introduce' qualifier to be useful.)_  <sub>added 2026-05-18 via detail:lessons-consolidate-dedup#4</sub>

- **Test-consolidation steps that defer all unit + integration tests from N prior implementation steps inherit the union of those steps' public surfaces. Create should size them by counting the testable APIs (function signatures × flag matrices × CLI mode matrices) added in steps 1..N-1, not by counting files in `files:`.** — Step 5 here was rated `high` and touched only 2 files, but the actual work was ~60 test methods spanning a pure parser, a checkpoint-cached primitive, a 3-way flag matrix on a public helper (`strict_check × warn_check × equivalence_invoker`), JSONL writer/reader, and a 6-mode CLI surface. A complexity rating based on file count would severely underestimate this.
